# Academy Awards

Annual awards for cinematic achievements

"Oscars" and "The Oscar" redirect here. For other uses, see [Oscar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oscar_\(disambiguation\) "Oscar (disambiguation)").

The **Academy Awards**, commonly known as the **Oscars**, are awards for [artistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artistic "Artistic") and technical merit in the [film industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Film_industry "Film industry").[\[1\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-history-1)[\[2\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-2) They are presented annually by the [Academy of Motion Picture Arts and Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_of_Motion_Picture_Arts_and_Sciences "Academy of Motion Picture Arts and Sciences") (AMPAS) in the [United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States "United States") in recognition of excellence in cinematic achievements as assessed by the Academy's voting membership.[\[3\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-3) The Oscars are widely considered to be the most prestigious awards in the film industry.[\[4\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-4)

The major award categories, known as the **Academy Awards of Merit**,[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-5) are presented during a live-televised [Hollywood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_the_United_States "Cinema of the United States") ceremony in February or March. It is the oldest worldwide entertainment awards ceremony.[\[1\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-history-1) The [1st Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1st_Academy_Awards "1st Academy Awards") were held in 1929.[\[6\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-6) The [second ceremony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2nd_Academy_Awards "2nd Academy Awards"), in 1930, was the first one broadcast by radio. The [1953 ceremony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/25th_Academy_Awards "25th Academy Awards") was the first one televised.[\[1\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-history-1) It is the oldest of the [four major annual American entertainment awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_EGOT_winners "List of EGOT winners"). Its counterparts—the [Emmy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmy_Awards "Emmy Awards") for television, the [Tony Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Awards "Tony Awards") for theater, and the [Grammy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grammy_Awards "Grammy Awards") for music—are modeled after the Academy Awards.[\[7\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-7) The Oscar statuette depicts a knight, rendered in the [Art Deco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Deco "Art Deco") style.[\[8\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-8)

## History

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Academy_Awards\&action=edit\&section=1 "Edit section: History")]

The [first Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1st_Academy_Awards "1st Academy Awards") presentation was held on May 16, 1929, at a private dinner function at [The Hollywood Roosevelt Hotel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Hollywood_Roosevelt_Hotel "The Hollywood Roosevelt Hotel"), with an audience of about 270 people.[\[9\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-9)

The post-awards party was held at the Mayfair Hotel.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-10) The cost of guest tickets for that night's ceremony was $5 (equivalent to $89 in 2023). Fifteen statuettes were awarded, honoring artists, directors, and other participants in the film-making industry of the time, for their works during the 1927–28 period. The ceremony ran for 15 minutes.

For this first ceremony, winners were announced to the media three months earlier.[\[11\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-11) For the second ceremony in 1930, and the rest of the first decade, the results were given to newspapers for publication at 11:00 pm on the night of the awards.[\[12\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-12) In 1940, the *[Los Angeles Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Times "Los Angeles Times")* announced the winners before the ceremony began. As a result, in 1941 the Academy started using a sealed envelope to reveal the names of the winners.[\[13\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-13)

The term "Oscar" is a registered trademark of the AMPAS.

### Milestones

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Academy_Awards\&action=edit\&section=2 "Edit section: Milestones")]

The first Best Actor awarded was [Emil Jannings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emil_Jannings "Emil Jannings"), for his performances in *[The Last Command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Last_Command_\(1928_film\) "The Last Command (1928 film)")* and *[The Way of All Flesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Way_of_All_Flesh_\(1927_film\) "The Way of All Flesh (1927 film)")*. As he had to return to Europe before the ceremony, the Academy agreed to give him the prize early, making him the first Academy Award recipient. For the [first Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1st_Academy_Awards "1st Academy Awards"), winners were recognized for multiple films during the qualifying period; Jannings received the award for two movies in which he starred, and [Janet Gaynor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janet_Gaynor "Janet Gaynor") won the first [Best Actress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Actress "Academy Award for Best Actress") award for performances in three films. Beginning with the second ceremony, performers received separate nominations for individual films; no performer has received multiple nominations in the same category since the [3rd Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/3rd_Academy_Awards "3rd Academy Awards").

For the first six ceremonies, the eligibility period ran from August 1 to July 31. The 6th Academy Awards' eligibility ran from August 1, 1932, to December 31, 1933, and as of the [7th Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/7th_Academy_Awards "7th Academy Awards"), subsequent eligibility periods have matched the calendar year (with the exception of the [93rd Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/93rd_Academy_Awards "93rd Academy Awards"), which, due to the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic "COVID-19 pandemic"), extended the eligibility period to February 28, 2021).[\[1\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-history-1)

Best Foreign Language Film, now known as [Best International Feature Film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_International_Feature_Film "Academy Award for Best International Feature Film"), was introduced at the [20th Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/20th_Academy_Awards "20th Academy Awards") as a special award, and became a competitive category at the [29th Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/29th_Academy_Awards "29th Academy Awards").[\[14\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-14).

The 74th Academy Awards, held in 2002, presented the first [Academy Award for Best Animated Feature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Animated_Feature "Academy Award for Best Animated Feature").[\[15\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-15)

Since 1973, all Academy Awards ceremonies, except for 2021, have ended with the [Academy Award for Best Picture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Picture "Academy Award for Best Picture"). Traditionally, the previous year's winners for Best Actor and Best Supporting Actor present the awards for Best Actress and Best Supporting Actress, respectively, while the previous year's winners for Best Actress and Best Supporting Actress present the awards for Best Actor and Best Supporting Actor. In 2009, this model was replaced by each acting award being introduced by five previous winners, each of whom introduces one of the nominated performances, referred to as the "Fab 5" presenters format.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-16) The Fab 5 model returned in 2024 after a 15-year hiatus.

On February 9, 2020, *[Parasite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parasite_\(2019_film\) "Parasite (2019 film)")* became the first foreign-language film to win Best Picture at the [92nd Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/92nd_Academy_Awards "92nd Academy Awards").[\[17\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-17)

The [93rd Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/93rd_Academy_Awards "93rd Academy Awards") ceremony, honoring the [best films of 2020](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_in_film "2020 in film") and [early 2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_in_film "2021 in film"), was held on April 25, 2021, after it was postponed from its original February 28, 2021, schedule due to the [impact of the COVID-19 pandemic on cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_on_cinema "Impact of the COVID-19 pandemic on cinema"). As with the two previous ceremonies, there was no host. The ceremony was broadcast on [ABC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Broadcasting_Company "American Broadcasting Company"). It took place at the [Dolby Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dolby_Theatre "Dolby Theatre") in Los Angeles, California for the 19th consecutive year, with satellite locations at [Union Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Station_\(Los_Angeles\) "Union Station (Los Angeles)") also in Los Angeles.[\[18\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-18) Because of the virus impact on films and TV industries, Academy president David Rubin and CEO Dawn Hudson announced that for the [2021 Oscar Ceremony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/93rd_Academy_Awards "93rd Academy Awards"), [streaming movies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Streaming_service_provider "Streaming service provider") with a previously planned theatrical release were eligible.[\[19\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-19) The theatrical requirement was reinstated starting with the [95th Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/95th_Academy_Awards "95th Academy Awards").[\[20\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-20)

## Oscar statuette

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Academy_Awards\&action=edit\&section=3 "Edit section: Oscar statuette")]

### Overview

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Academy_Awards\&action=edit\&section=4 "Edit section: Overview")]

The Oscar statuette, officially the Academy Award of Merit,[\[21\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-statuette-21) is given to winners of each year's awards. Made of gold-plated [bronze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze "Bronze") on a black metal base, it is 13.5 in (34.3 cm) tall, weighs 8.5 lb (3.9 kg) and depicts a knight rendered in [Art Deco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Deco "Art Deco") style holding a sword standing on a reel of film with five spokes. The five spokes represent the original branches of the Academy: Actors, Writers, Directors, Producers, and Technicians.[\[22\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-22)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5e/DSC_7227_%D0%9C%D1%83%D0%B7%D0%B5%D0%B9_%D0%BA%D0%B8%D0%BD%D0%BE.jpg/200px-DSC_7227_%D0%9C%D1%83%D0%B7%D0%B5%D0%B9_%D0%BA%D0%B8%D0%BD%D0%BE.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:DSC_7227_%D0%9C%D1%83%D0%B7%D0%B5%D0%B9_%D0%BA%D0%B8%D0%BD%D0%BE.jpg)

Plaster War-time Oscar plaque (1943), State Central Museum of Cinema, Moscow [(ru)](https://ru.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D0%93%D0%BE%D1%81%D1%83%D0%B4%D0%B0%D1%80%D1%81%D1%82%D0%B2%D0%B5%D0%BD%D0%BD%D1%8B%D0%B9_%D1%86%D0%B5%D0%BD%D1%82%D1%80%D0%B0%D0%BB%D1%8C%D0%BD%D1%8B%D0%B9_%D0%BC%D1%83%D0%B7%D0%B5%D0%B9_%D0%BA%D0%B8%D0%BD%D0%BE "ru:Государственный центральный музей кино")

Sculptor [George Stanley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Stanley_\(sculptor\) "George Stanley (sculptor)"), who also did the Muse Fountain at the [Hollywood Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hollywood_Bowl "Hollywood Bowl"), sculpted [Cedric Gibbons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cedric_Gibbons "Cedric Gibbons")' design. The statuettes presented at the initial ceremonies were gold-plated solid bronze. Within a few years, the bronze was abandoned in favor of [Britannia metal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Britannia_metal "Britannia metal"), a pewter-like alloy which is then plated in copper, nickel silver, and finally, 24-karat gold.[\[21\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-statuette-21) Due to a metal shortage during World War II, Oscars were made of painted plaster for three years. Following the war, the Academy invited recipients to redeem the plaster figures for gold-plated metal ones.[\[23\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-23)

The only addition to the Oscar since it was created is a minor streamlining of the base. The original Oscar mold was cast in 1928 at the [C.W. Shumway & Sons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C.W._Shumway_%26_Sons "C.W. Shumway & Sons") Foundry in [Batavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batavia,_Illinois "Batavia, Illinois"), Illinois, which also contributed to casting the molds for the [Vince Lombardi Trophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vince_Lombardi_Trophy "Vince Lombardi Trophy") and [Emmy Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmy_Award "Emmy Award") statuettes. During the 1970s, the Oscar statues were cast in [Crystal Lake, Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crystal_Lake,_Illinois "Crystal Lake, Illinois"). [\[24\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-24) From 1983 to 2015,[\[25\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-25) approximately 50 Oscars in a tin alloy with gold plating were made each year in Chicago by Illinois manufacturer [R.S. Owens & Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R.S._Owens_%26_Company "R.S. Owens & Company").[\[26\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-26) It would take between three and four weeks to manufacture 50 statuettes.[\[27\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:0-27)

In 2016, the Academy returned to bronze as the core metal of the statuettes, handing manufacturing duties to [Walden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walden,_New_York "Walden, New York"), New York-based Polich Tallix Fine Art Foundry, now owned and operated by [UAP Urban Art Projects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_Art_Projects "Urban Art Projects").[\[28\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Oscarstatuettes2016-28)[\[29\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-29) While based on a digital scan of an original 1929 Oscar, the statuettes retain their modern-era dimensions and black pedestal. Cast in liquid bronze from [3D-printed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/3D-printed "3D-printed") ceramic molds and polished, they are then electroplated in 24-karat gold by [Brooklyn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brooklyn,_New_York "Brooklyn, New York"), New York-based Epner Technology. The time required to produce 50 such statuettes is roughly three months.[\[30\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-30) R.S. Owens is expected to continue producing other awards for the Academy, and service existing Oscars that need replating.[\[31\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-31)

### Naming

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Academy_Awards\&action=edit\&section=5 "Edit section: Naming")]

The origin of the nickname of the trophy has been disputed, as multiple people have taken credit for naming the trophy "Oscar".

[Margaret Herrick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margaret_Herrick "Margaret Herrick"), librarian and president of the Academy, may have said she named it after her supposed uncle Oscar in 1921.[\[a\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-32) The only corroboration was a 1938 clipping from the *[Los Angeles Examiner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Examiner "Los Angeles Examiner")*, in which Herrick told a story of her and her husband joking with each other using the phrase, "How's your uncle Oscar".[\[32\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:2-33)

[Bette Davis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bette_Davis "Bette Davis"), in her 1962 autobiography, claimed she named it in 1936 after her first husband, Harmon Oscar Nelson, of whom the statue's rear end reminded her.[\[32\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:2-33)[\[33\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-34) But the term had been in use at least two years before. In a 1974 biography written by Whitney Stine with commentary from Davis, Davis wrote "I relinquish once and for all any claim that I was the one—so, Academy of Motion Picture Arts and Sciences, the honor is all yours".[\[32\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:2-33)[\[34\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-35)

Columnist [Sidney Skolsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sidney_Skolsky "Sidney Skolsky") wrote in his 1970 memoir that he came up with the term in 1934 under pressure for a deadline, mocking [Vaudeville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaudeville "Vaudeville") comedians who asked "Will you have a cigar, Oscar?" The Academy credits Skolsky with "the first confirmed newspaper reference" to *Oscar* in his column on March 16, 1934, which was written about that year's [6th Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/6th_Academy_Awards "6th Academy Awards").[\[35\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-36) But in the newspaper clipping that Skolsky referred to, he wrote that "these statues are called 'Oscars'", meaning that the name was already in use.[\[32\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:2-33)

Bruce Davis, a former executive director of the Academy, credited Eleanore Lilleberg, a secretary at the Academy when the award was first introduced, for the nickname. She had overseen the pre-ceremony handling of the awards. Davis credits Lilleberg because he found in an autobiography of Einar Lilleberg, Eleanore's brother, that Einar had referenced a Norwegian army veteran named Oscar that the two knew in Chicago, whom Einar described as having always "stood straight and tall".[\[32\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:2-33)[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:3-37) He asserts credit "should almost certainly belong to" Lilleberg.[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:3-37)

In 2021, Brazilian researcher Dr. [Waldemar Dalenogare Neto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waldemar_Dalenogare_Neto "Waldemar Dalenogare Neto") found the probable first public mention of the name "Oscar", in journalist [Relman Morin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relman_Morin "Relman Morin")'s "Cinematters" column in the *[Los Angeles Evening Post-Record](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Evening_Post-Record "Los Angeles Evening Post-Record")* on December 5, 1933. Since the awards didn't take place that year, he said: "What's happened to the annual Academy banquet? As a rule, the banquet and the awarding of "Oscar", the bronze statuette given for best performances, is all over long before this". This information changes the version of Sidney Skolsky as the first to publicly mention the name.[\[37\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-38)

### Engraving

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Academy_Awards\&action=edit\&section=6 "Edit section: Engraving")]

To prevent information identifying the Oscar winners from leaking ahead of the ceremony, Oscar statuettes presented at the ceremony have blank baseplates. Until 2010, winners returned their statuettes to the Academy and had to wait several weeks to have their names inscribed on their respective Oscars. Since 2010, winners have had the option of having engraved nameplates applied to their statuettes at an inscription-processing station at the Governor's Ball, a party held immediately after the Oscar ceremony. The R.S. Owens company has engraved nameplates made before the ceremony, bearing the name of every potential winner. The nameplates for the non-winning nominees are later recycled.[\[38\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-39)[\[39\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-40)

### Ownership of Oscar statuettes

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Academy_Awards\&action=edit\&section=7 "Edit section: Ownership of Oscar statuettes")]

Prior to 1950, Oscar statuettes were, and remain, the property of the recipient.[\[40\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-41) Since then the statuettes have been legally [encumbered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Encumbrance "Encumbrance") by the requirement that the statuette be first offered for sale back to the Academy for $1. If a winner refuses to agree to this stipulation, then the Academy keeps the statuette. Academy Awards predating this agreement have been sold in public auctions and private deals for six-figure sums.[\[41\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-money.com-42)

In 1989, [Michael Todd's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Todd "Mike Todd") grandson tried to sell Todd's [Best Picture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Picture "Academy Award for Best Picture") Oscar for his 1956 production of *[Around the World in 80 Days](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Around_the_World_in_80_Days_\(1956_film\) "Around the World in 80 Days (1956 film)")* to a movie prop collector. The Academy earned enforcement of its statuette contract by gaining a permanent injunction against the sale.

In 1992, [Harold Russell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harold_Russell "Harold Russell") consigned his 1946 Oscar for Best Supporting Actor for *[The Best Years of Our Lives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Best_Years_of_Our_Lives "The Best Years of Our Lives")* to auction to raise money for his wife's medical expenses. Though his decision caused controversy, the first Oscar ever to be sold passed to a private collector on August 6, 1992, for $60,500 (equivalent to $131,358 in 2023). Russell defended his action, saying, "I don't know why anybody would be critical. My wife's health is much more important than sentimental reasons. The movie will be here, even if Oscar isn't".[\[42\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-43)

In December 2011, [Orson Welles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orson_Welles "Orson Welles")' 1941 Oscar for *[Citizen Kane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citizen_Kane "Citizen Kane")* ([Academy Award for Best Original Screenplay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Original_Screenplay "Academy Award for Best Original Screenplay")) was put up for auction, after his heirs won a 2004 court decision contending that Welles did not sign any agreement to return the statue to the Academy.[\[43\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-44) On December 20, 2011, it sold in an online auction for $861,542 (equivalent to $1,166,905 in 2023).[\[44\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Welles'_Oscar_sold-45)

Some buyers have subsequently returned the statuettes to the Academy, which keeps them in its treasury.[\[41\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-money.com-42)

### Other awards presented by the Academy

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Academy_Awards\&action=edit\&section=8 "Edit section: Other awards presented by the Academy")]

In addition to the Academy Award of Merit (Oscar award), there are nine honorary (non-competitive) awards presented by the Academy from time to time (except for the Academy Honorary Award, the Technical Achievement Award, and the Student Academy Awards, which are presented annually):[\[45\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-46)

- [Governors Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governors_Awards "Governors Awards"):

  - The [Academy Honorary Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Honorary_Award "Academy Honorary Award") (annual) (which may or may not be in the form of an Oscar statuette);
  - The [Irving G. Thalberg Memorial Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irving_G._Thalberg_Memorial_Award "Irving G. Thalberg Memorial Award") (since 1938) (in the form of a bust of Thalberg);
  - The [Jean Hersholt Humanitarian Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Hersholt_Humanitarian_Award "Jean Hersholt Humanitarian Award") (since 1957) (in the form of an Oscar statuette);

- The [Academy Scientific and Technical Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Scientific_and_Technical_Award "Academy Scientific and Technical Award"):

  - Academy Award of Merit (non-competitive) (in the form of an Oscar statuette);
  - [Scientific and Engineering Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_and_Engineering_Award "Scientific and Engineering Award") (in the form of a bronze tablet);
  - [Technical Achievement Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Technical_Achievement "Academy Award for Technical Achievement") (annual) (in the form of a certificate);
  - The [John A. Bonner Medal of Commendation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_A._Bonner_Medal_of_Commendation "John A. Bonner Medal of Commendation") (since 1978) (in the form of a medal);
  - The [Gordon E. Sawyer Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gordon_E._Sawyer_Award "Gordon E. Sawyer Award") (since 1982); and

- The Academy [Student Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Student_Academy_Awards "Student Academy Awards") (annual).

The Academy also awards [Nicholl Fellowships in Screenwriting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicholl_Fellowships_in_Screenwriting "Nicholl Fellowships in Screenwriting").

## Nomination

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Academy_Awards\&action=edit\&section=9 "Edit section: Nomination")]

From 2004 to 2020, the Academy Award nomination results were announced to the public in mid-January. Prior to that, the results were announced in early February. In 2021, the nominees were announced in March. In 2022, the nominees were announced in early February for the first time since 2003.

### Voters

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Academy_Awards\&action=edit\&section=10 "Edit section: Voters")]

The Academy of Motion Picture Arts and Sciences (AMPAS), a professional honorary organization, is composed of 9,905 voting members as of 2024[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Academy_Awards\&action=edit).[\[46\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-47)[\[47\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Pond-48)

Academy membership is divided into different branches, with each representing a different discipline in film production. As of 2024[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Academy_Awards\&action=edit), actors constitute the largest bloc, numbering 1,258 (12.7% of the voting body).[\[47\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Pond-48) Votes have been certified by the auditing firm [PricewaterhouseCoopers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PricewaterhouseCoopers "PricewaterhouseCoopers"), and its predecessor [Price Waterhouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Price_Waterhouse "Price Waterhouse"), since the [7th Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/7th_Academy_Awards "7th Academy Awards") in 1935.[\[48\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-49)[\[49\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-50)[\[50\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-51) In May 2011, the Academy sent a letter advising its then-6,000 or so voting members that an online system for Oscar voting would be implemented in 2013, replacing mailed paper ballots.[\[51\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-52)

All AMPAS members must be invited to join by the Board of Governors, on behalf of Academy Branch Executive Committees. Membership eligibility may be achieved by a competitive nomination, or an existing member may submit a name, based on other significant contributions to the field of motion pictures.

New membership proposals are considered annually. The Academy does not publicly disclose its membership, although as recently as 2007 press releases have announced the names of those who have been invited to join.[\[52\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-53)

In 2012, the results of a study conducted by the *[Los Angeles Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Times "Los Angeles Times")* were published describing the demographic breakdown of approximately 88% of AMPAS' voting membership. Of the 5,100+ active voters confirmed, 94% were [Caucasian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasian_race "Caucasian race"), 77% were male, and 54% were found to be over the age of 60. 33% of voting members are former nominees (14%) and winners (19%).[\[53\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-54) In 2016, the Academy launched an initiative to expand its membership and increase diversity. In 2024, voting membership stood at 9,905.[\[47\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Pond-48)

### Rules

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Academy_Awards\&action=edit\&section=11 "Edit section: Rules")]

According to Rules 2 and 3 of the official Academy Awards Rules, a film must open in the previous calendar year, from midnight at the start of January 1 to midnight at the end of December 31, in [Los Angeles County, California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_County,_California "Los Angeles County, California"), and play for seven consecutive days, to qualify, except for the Best International Feature Film, Best Documentary Feature, and awards in short film categories. The film must be shown at least three times on each day of its qualifying run, with at least one of the daily showings starting between 6 pm and 10 pm local time.[\[54\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-92Rule2-55)[\[55\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-56)

For example, the 2009 [Best Picture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Picture "Academy Award for Best Picture") winner, *[The Hurt Locker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Hurt_Locker "The Hurt Locker")*, was originally first released in 2008, but did not qualify for the [2008 awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/81st_Academy_Awards "81st Academy Awards"), as it did not play its Oscar-qualifying run in Los Angeles until mid-2009, thus qualifying for the [2009 awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/82nd_Academy_Awards "82nd Academy Awards"). Foreign films must include English subtitles. Each country can submit only one film for consideration in the International Feature Film category per year.[\[56\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-57)

Rule 2 states that a film must be [feature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feature_film "Feature film")-length, defined as a minimum of 40 minutes, except for short-subject awards. It must exist either on a [35 mm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/35mm_movie_film "35mm movie film") or [70 mm film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/70_mm_film "70 mm film") print, or in 24 frame/s or 48 frame/s [progressive scan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_scan "Progressive scan") [digital cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digital_cinema "Digital cinema") format, with a minimum projector resolution of 2,048 by 1,080 pixels.[\[57\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-58) Since the [90th Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/90th_Academy_Awards "90th Academy Awards"), presented in 2018, multi-part and limited series have been ineligible for the Best Documentary Feature award. This followed the win of *[O.J.: Made in America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/O.J.:_Made_in_America "O.J.: Made in America")*, an eight-hour presentation that was screened in a limited release before being broadcast in five parts on [ABC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Broadcasting_Company "American Broadcasting Company") and [ESPN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ESPN "ESPN"), in that category in [2017](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/89th_Academy_Awards "89th Academy Awards"). The Academy's announcement of the new rule made no direct mention of that film.[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:3-37)

The Best International Feature Film award does not require a U.S. release. It requires the film to be submitted as its country's official selection.

The Best Documentary Feature award requires either week-long releases in *both* Los Angeles County *and* any of the [five boroughs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boroughs_of_New_York_City "Boroughs of New York City") of New York City during the previous calendar year,[\[b\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-60) or a qualifying award at a competitive film festival from the Documentary Feature Qualifying Festival list, regardless of any public exhibition or distribution, or submission in the International Feature Film category as its country's official selection. The qualifying theatrical runs must meet the same requirements as those for non-documentary films regarding numbers and times of screenings. A film must have been reviewed by a critic from *[The New York Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times "The New York Times")*, *[Time Out New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_Out_New_York "Time Out New York")*, the *[Los Angeles Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Times "Los Angeles Times")*, or *[LA Weekly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LA_Weekly "LA Weekly")*.[\[59\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-92Rule11-61)

Producers must submit an Official Screen Credits online form before the deadline. If it is not submitted by the defined deadline, the film will be ineligible for Academy Awards in any year. The form includes the production credits for all related categories.

Awards in short film categories (Best Documentary Short Subject, Best Animated Short Film, and Best Live Action Short Film) have different eligibility rules from most other competitive awards. First, the qualifying period for release does not coincide with a calendar year, instead covering one year starting on October 1, and ending on September 30 of the calendar year before the ceremony. Second, there are multiple methods of qualification. The main method is a week-long theatrical release in *either* New York City *or* Los Angeles County during the eligibility period. Films also can qualify by winning specified awards at one of several competitive film festivals designated by the Academy, also without regard to prior public distribution.[\[59\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-92Rule11-61)[\[60\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-92Rule19-62)

A film that is selected as a gold, silver, or bronze medal winner in an appropriate category of the immediately previous Student Academy Awards is also eligible (Documentary category for that award, and Animation, Narrative, Alternative, or International for the other awards). The requirements for the qualifying theatrical run are also different from those for other awards. Only one screening per day is required. For the Documentary award, the screening must start between noon and 10 pm local time. For other awards, no specific start time is required, but the film must appear in regular theater listings with dates and screening times.[\[59\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-92Rule11-61)[\[60\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-92Rule19-62)

In late December, ballots and lists of eligible films are sent to the membership. For most categories, members from each of the branches vote to determine the nominees only in their respective categories, i.e. only directors vote for directors, writers for writers, actors for actors, etc. In the special case of Best Picture, all voting members are eligible to select the nominees. A number of branches are only eligible to vote in Best Picture during nomination voting; this includes a producers' branch, as Best Picture is awarded to a film's producer(s), and other branches which have no corresponding award.[\[47\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Pond-48) In all major categories, a variant of the [single transferable vote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Single_transferable_vote "Single transferable vote") is used, with each member casting a ballot with up to five nominees (ten for Best Picture) ranked preferentially.[\[61\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-TheEnvelope-63)[\[62\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-64)[\[63\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-vox-65) In certain categories, including International Feature Film, Documentary and Animated Feature, nominees are selected by special screening committees made up of members from all branches.

In most categories, the winner is selected from among the nominees by [plurality voting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plurality_voting "Plurality voting") of all members.[\[61\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-TheEnvelope-63)[\[63\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-vox-65) Since 2009, the Best Picture winner has been chosen by [instant runoff voting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instant_runoff_voting "Instant runoff voting").[\[63\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-vox-65)[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-66) Since 2013, re-weighted [range voting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Range_voting "Range voting") has been used to select the nominees for the [Best Visual Effects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Visual_Effects "Academy Award for Best Visual Effects").[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-67)

Film companies will spend as much as several million dollars on [marketing to awards voters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/For_Your_Consideration_\(advertising\) "For Your Consideration (advertising)") for a movie in the running for Best Picture, in attempts to improve chances of receiving Oscars and other movie awards conferred in [Oscar season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oscar_season "Oscar season"). The Academy enforces rules to limit overt campaigning by its members to try to eliminate excesses and prevent the process from becoming undignified. It has an awards czar on staff who advises members on allowed practices and levies penalties on offenders.[\[66\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-68) For example, a producer of the 2009 Best Picture nominee *[The Hurt Locker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Hurt_Locker "The Hurt Locker")* was disqualified as a producer in the category when he contacted associates urging them to vote for his film and not another that was seen as the front-runner. *The Hurt Locker* eventually won.

### Academy Screening Room

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Academy_Awards\&action=edit\&section=12 "Edit section: Academy Screening Room")]

The Academy Screening Room or Academy Digital Screening Room is a secure [streaming platform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_streaming_media_services "List of streaming media services") which allows voting members of the Academy to view all eligible films (except, initially, those in the International category) in one place. It was introduced in 2019, for the 2020 Oscars. DVD screeners and Academy in-person screenings were still provided. For films to be included on the platform, the North American distributor must pay $12,500, including a [watermarking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digital_watermarking "Digital watermarking") fee, and a digital copy of the film to be prepared for streaming by the Academy. The platform can be accessed via [Apple TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple_TV "Apple TV") and [Roku](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roku "Roku") players.[\[67\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-69)[\[68\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-70) The watermarking process involved several video security firms, creating a forensic watermark and restricting the ability to take screenshots or screen recordings.[\[69\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-71)

In 2021, for the 2022 Oscars, the Academy banned all physical screeners and in-person screenings, restricting official membership viewing to the Academy Screening Room. Films eligible in the Documentary and International categories were made available in different sections of the platform. Distributors can also pay an extra fee to add video featurettes to promote their films on the platform.[\[70\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-DeadlineScreeningRoom-72) The in-person screenings were said to be cancelled because of the COVID-19 pandemic.[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-73) Eligible films do not have to be added to the platform, but the Academy advertises them to voting members when they are.[\[70\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-DeadlineScreeningRoom-72)

## Awards ceremonies

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Academy_Awards\&action=edit\&section=13 "Edit section: Awards ceremonies")]

See also: [List of Academy Awards ceremonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Academy_Awards_ceremonies "List of Academy Awards ceremonies")

### Telecast

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Academy_Awards\&action=edit\&section=14 "Edit section: Telecast")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d6/31st_Acad_Awards.jpg/220px-31st_Acad_Awards.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:31st_Acad_Awards.jpg)

The [31st Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/31st_Academy_Awards "31st Academy Awards"), [Hollywood Pantages Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hollywood_Pantages_Theatre "Hollywood Pantages Theatre"), 1959

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d9/81st_Academy_Awards_Ceremony.JPG/220px-81st_Academy_Awards_Ceremony.JPG)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:81st_Academy_Awards_Ceremony.JPG)

The [81st Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/81st_Academy_Awards "81st Academy Awards"), [Dolby Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dolby_Theatre "Dolby Theatre"), 2009

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/Dolby_Theatre_Oscars_Los_Angeles_USA_Mar23_IMG_8321.jpg/220px-Dolby_Theatre_Oscars_Los_Angeles_USA_Mar23_IMG_8321.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Dolby_Theatre_Oscars_Los_Angeles_USA_Mar23_IMG_8321.jpg)

The [95th Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/95th_Academy_Awards "95th Academy Awards"), [Dolby Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dolby_Theatre "Dolby Theatre"), 2023

The major awards are presented at a live televised ceremony, commonly in late February or early March following the relevant calendar year, and six weeks after the announcement of the nominees. It is the culmination of the film awards season, which usually begins during November or December of the previous year. This is an elaborate extravaganza, with the invited guests walking up the red carpet in the creations of the most prominent fashion designers of the day. [Black tie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_tie "Black tie") dress is the most common outfit for men. Fashion may dictate not wearing a [bow-tie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bow-tie "Bow-tie"), and musical performers are sometimes not required to adhere to this. The artists who recorded the nominees for Best Original Song quite often perform those songs live at the awards ceremony, and the fact that they are performing is often used to promote the television broadcast.

The Academy Awards is the world's longest-running awards show televised live from the United States to all time zones in North America and worldwide, and gathers billions of viewers elsewhere throughout the world.[\[72\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-74) The Oscars were first televised [in 1953](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/25th_Academy_Awards "25th Academy Awards") by [NBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBC "NBC"), which continued to broadcast the event until [1960](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/32nd_Academy_Awards "32nd Academy Awards"), when [ABC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Broadcasting_Company "American Broadcasting Company") took over, televising the festivities, including the first color broadcast of the event in [1966](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/38th_Academy_Awards "38th Academy Awards"), to [1970](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/42nd_Academy_Awards "42nd Academy Awards"). NBC regained the rights for five years (1971–75), then ABC resumed broadcast duties in [1976](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/48th_Academy_Awards "48th Academy Awards") and its current contract with the Academy runs through 2028.[\[73\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-75)

The Academy has produced condensed versions of the ceremony for broadcast in international markets, especially those outside of the Americas, in more desirable local timeslots. The ceremony was broadcast live internationally for the first time via satellite since 1970, but only two South American countries, Chile and Brazil, purchased the rights to air the broadcast. By that time, the television rights to the Academy Awards had been sold in 50 countries. In 1980, the rights were sold to 60 countries, and by [1984](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/56th_Academy_Awards "56th Academy Awards"), the television rights to the Academy Awards were licensed in 76 countries.

[In 2004](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/76th_Academy_Awards "76th Academy Awards"), the ceremonies were moved up from late March/early April to late February, to help disrupt and shorten the intense [lobbying and ad campaigns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/For_Your_Consideration_\(advertising\) "For Your Consideration (advertising)") associated with [Oscar season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oscar_season "Oscar season") in the film industry. Another reason was because of the growing television ratings success coinciding with the [NCAA division I men's basketball tournament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA_Division_I_men%27s_basketball_tournament "NCAA Division I men's basketball tournament"), which would cut into the Academy Awards audience. In [1976](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/48th_Academy_Awards "48th Academy Awards") and [1977](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/49th_Academy_Awards "49th Academy Awards"), ABC's regained Oscars were moved from Tuesday to Monday and went directly opposite the national championship game on NBC. The earlier date is also to the advantage of ABC, as it now usually occurs during the highly profitable and important February [sweeps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sweeps "Sweeps") period.[\[74\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Fang-76)

Some years, the ceremony is moved into the first Sunday of March to avoid a clash with the [Winter Olympic Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winter_Olympic_Games "Winter Olympic Games"). Another reason for the move to late February and early March is to avoid the awards ceremony occurring so close to the religious holidays of [Passover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passover "Passover") and Easter, which for decades had been a grievance from members and the general public.[\[74\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Fang-76) Advertising is somewhat restricted, as traditionally no movie studios or competitors of official Academy Award sponsors may advertise during the telecast. As of 2020, the production of the Academy Awards telecast held the distinction of winning one the highest number of [Emmys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmy_Awards "Emmy Awards") in history, with 54 wins and 280 nominations overall.[\[75\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-77)

After many years of being held on Mondays at 6:00 p.m. [Pacific](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Time_Zone "Pacific Time Zone")/9:00 pm [Eastern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Time_Zone "Eastern Time Zone"), since the [1999 ceremonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/71st_Academy_Awards "71st Academy Awards"), it was moved to Sundays at 5:30 pm PT/8:30 pm ET.[\[76\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-78) The reasons given for the move were that more viewers would tune in on Sundays, that Los Angeles rush-hour traffic jams could be avoided, and an earlier start time would allow viewers on the East Coast to go to bed earlier.[\[77\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-79) For many years the film industry opposed a Sunday broadcast because it would cut into the weekend box office.[\[78\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-80)

In 2010, the Academy contemplated moving the ceremony even further back into January, citing television viewers' fatigue with the film industry's long awards season. However, such an accelerated schedule would dramatically decrease the voting period for its members, to the point where some voters would only have time to view the contending films streamed on their computers, as opposed to traditionally receiving the films and ballots in the mail. Furthermore, a January ceremony on Sunday would clash with [National Football League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Football_League "National Football League") (NFL) playoff games.[\[79\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-81) In 2018, the Academy announced that the ceremony would be moved from late February to mid-February beginning with the 92nd Academy Awards in 2020.[\[80\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-82) In 2024, the ceremony was moved to an even earlier start time of 4:00 pm PT/7:00 p.m. ET, the apparent impetus being the ability for ABC to air a half-hour of primetime programming as a lead-out program at 7:30 p.m. PT/10:30 p.m. ET.[\[81\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-83)

Originally scheduled for April 8, 1968, the [40th Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/40th_Academy_Awards "40th Academy Awards") ceremony was postponed for two days, because of the [assassination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Martin_Luther_King_Jr. "Assassination of Martin Luther King Jr.") of [Dr. Martin Luther King, Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Luther_King_Jr. "Martin Luther King Jr.") On March 30, 1981, the [53rd Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/53rd_Academy_Awards "53rd Academy Awards") was postponed for one day, after the [attempted assassination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attempted_assassination_of_Ronald_Reagan "Attempted assassination of Ronald Reagan") of President [Ronald Reagan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ronald_Reagan "Ronald Reagan") and others in Washington, D.C.[\[82\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-84)

In 1993, an *In Memoriam* segment was introduced,[\[83\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-fawcett-85) honoring those who had made a significant contribution to cinema who had died in the preceding 12 months, a selection compiled by a small committee of Academy members.[\[84\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-86) This segment has drawn criticism over the years for the omission of some names. Criticism was also levied for many years regarding another aspect, with the segment having a "[popularity contest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popularity_contest "Popularity contest")" feel as the audience varied their applause to those who had died by the subject's cultural impact. The applause has since been muted during the telecast, and the audience is discouraged from clapping during the segment and giving silent reflection instead. This segment was later followed by a commercial break.

In terms of broadcast length, the ceremony generally averages three and a half hours. The first Oscars, in 1929, lasted 15 minutes. At the other end of the spectrum, the 2002 ceremony lasted four hours and twenty-three minutes.[\[85\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-87)[\[86\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-88) In 2010, the organizers of the Academy Awards announced winners' [acceptance speeches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oscar_speech "Oscar speech") must not run past 45 seconds. This, according to organizer Bill Mechanic, was to ensure the elimination of what he termed "the single most hated thing on the show"—overly long and embarrassing displays of emotion.[\[87\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-89) In 2016, in a further effort to streamline speeches, winners' dedications were displayed on an on-screen [ticker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/News_ticker "News ticker").[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-usatoday-thankyouscroll-90)

During the 2018 ceremony, host [Jimmy Kimmel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Kimmel "Jimmy Kimmel") acknowledged how long the ceremony had become, by announcing that he would give a brand-new [jet ski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jet_ski "Jet ski") to whoever gave the shortest speech of the night, a reward won by [Mark Bridges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Bridges_\(costume_designer\) "Mark Bridges (costume designer)") when accepting his [Best Costume Design](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Costume_Design "Academy Award for Best Costume Design") award for *[Phantom Thread](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phantom_Thread "Phantom Thread")*.[\[89\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-91) *[The Wall Street Journal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Wall_Street_Journal "The Wall Street Journal")* analyzed the average minutes spent across the 2014–2018 telecasts as follows: 14 on song performances; 25 on the hosts' speeches; 38 on prerecorded clips; and 78 on the awards themselves, broken into 24 on the introduction and announcement, 24 on winners walking to the stage, and 30 on their acceptance speeches.[\[90\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-92)

Although still dominant in ratings, the viewership of the Academy Awards has steadily dropped. The [88th Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/88th_Academy_Awards "88th Academy Awards") were the lowest-rated in the past eight years (although with increases in male and 18–49 viewership), while the show itself also faced mixed reception. Following the show, *[Variety](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Variety_\(magazine\) "Variety (magazine)")* reported that ABC was, in negotiating an extension to its contract to broadcast the Oscars, seeking to have more creative control over the broadcast itself. Currently and nominally, AMPAS is responsible for most aspects of the telecast, including the choice of production staff and hosting, although ABC is allowed to have some input on their decisions.[\[91\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-variety-struggleabc-93) In August 2016, AMPAS extended its contract with ABC to 2028: the contract neither contains any notable changes nor gives ABC any further creative control over the telecast.[\[92\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-variety-2028-94)

### TV ratings

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Academy_Awards\&action=edit\&section=15 "Edit section: TV ratings")]

Historically, the telecast's viewership is higher when box-office hits are favored to win the Best Picture award. More than 57.25 million viewers tuned to the telecast for the [70th Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/70th_Academy_Awards "70th Academy Awards") in 1998, the year of *[Titanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanic_\(1997_film\) "Titanic (1997 film)")*, which generated a box office haul during its initial 1997–98 run of $600.8 million in the US, a box-office record that would remain unsurpassed for years.[\[93\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-95) The [76th Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/76th_Academy_Awards "76th Academy Awards") ceremony, in which *[The Lord of the Rings: The Return of the King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Lord_of_the_Rings:_The_Return_of_the_King "The Lord of the Rings: The Return of the King")* (pre-telecast box office earnings of $368 million) received 11 Awards, including Best Picture, drew 43.56 million viewers.[\[94\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-usatoday_oscar-96) The most-watched ceremony based on [Nielsen ratings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nielsen_ratings "Nielsen ratings") to date, was the [42nd Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/42nd_Academy_Awards "42nd Academy Awards") (Best Picture *[Midnight Cowboy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midnight_Cowboy "Midnight Cowboy")*), which drew a 43.4% household rating on April 7, 1970.[\[95\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-97) Hoping to reinvigorate the pre-show and ratings, the 2023 Oscars organizers hired members of the [Met Gala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Met_Gala "Met Gala") creative team.[\[96\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-OscarsHiredMetGalaTeam-98)

By contrast, ceremonies honoring films that have not performed well at the box office tend to show weaker ratings, despite how much critical acclaim those films have received. The [78th Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/78th_Academy_Awards "78th Academy Awards"), which awarded a low-budget independent film (*[Crash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crash_\(2004_film\) "Crash (2004 film)")* with a pre-Oscar gross of $53.4 million) generated an audience of 38.64 million with a household rating of 22.91%.[\[97\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-99) In 2008, the [80th Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/80th_Academy_Awards "80th Academy Awards") telecast was watched by 31.76 million viewers on average with an 18.66% household rating, the lowest-rated and least-watched ceremony at the time, in spite of celebrating 80 years of the Academy Awards.[\[98\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-100) The Best Picture winner of that particular ceremony was another independent film (this time, the [Coen brothers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coen_brothers "Coen brothers")'s *[No Country for Old Men](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No_Country_for_Old_Men "No Country for Old Men")*).

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/Academy_Awards_Viewership_1974-2023%2C_in_millions.png/220px-Academy_Awards_Viewership_1974-2023%2C_in_millions.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Academy_Awards_Viewership_1974-2023,_in_millions.png)

Academy Awards Viewership 1974–2023, in millions[\[99\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-tvbythenumbers-101)

Whereas the [92nd Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/92nd_Academy_Awards "92nd Academy Awards") drew an average of 23.6 million viewers,[\[100\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-102) the [93rd Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/93rd_Academy_Awards "93rd Academy Awards") drew an even lower viewership of 10.4 million,[\[101\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-103) the lowest viewership recorded by Nielsen since it started recording audience totals in 1974.[\[102\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-104) The [94th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/94th_Academy_Awards "94th Academy Awards") and [95th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/95th_Academy_Awards "95th Academy Awards") editions drew 16.6 and 18.7 million viewers, respectively, still below the audience of the 92nd edition.[\[103\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-CNN_Ratings_2022-105)[\[104\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Yahoo_Ratings_2023-106)

### Archive

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Academy_Awards\&action=edit\&section=16 "Edit section: Archive")]

The [Academy Film Archive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Film_Archive "Academy Film Archive") holds copies of every Academy Awards ceremony since the 1949 Oscars, as well as material on many prior ceremonies, along with ancillary material related to more recent shows. Copies are held in a variety of film, video and digital formats.[\[105\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-107)

## Venues

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Academy_Awards\&action=edit\&section=17 "Edit section: Venues")]

In 1929, the first Academy Awards were presented at a banquet dinner at [The Hollywood Roosevelt Hotel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Hollywood_Roosevelt_Hotel "The Hollywood Roosevelt Hotel"). From 1930 to 1943, the ceremony alternated between two venues: the [Ambassador Hotel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambassador_Hotel_\(Los_Angeles\) "Ambassador Hotel (Los Angeles)") on [Wilshire Boulevard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilshire_Boulevard "Wilshire Boulevard") and the [Biltmore Hotel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millennium_Biltmore_Hotel "Millennium Biltmore Hotel") in downtown Los Angeles.

[Grauman's Chinese Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grauman%27s_Chinese_Theatre "Grauman's Chinese Theatre") in Hollywood then hosted the awards from 1944 to 1946, followed by the [Shrine Auditorium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shrine_Auditorium "Shrine Auditorium") in Los Angeles from 1947 to 1948. The [21st Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/21st_Academy_Awards "21st Academy Awards") in 1949 were held at the Academy Award Theatre at what had been the Academy's headquarters on [Melrose Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melrose_Avenue "Melrose Avenue") in Hollywood.[\[106\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-108)

From 1950 to 1960, the awards were presented at Hollywood's [Pantages Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pantages_Theatre_\(Hollywood\) "Pantages Theatre (Hollywood)"). With the advent of television, the awards from 1953 to 1957 took place simultaneously in Hollywood and New York, first at the [NBC International Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majestic_Theatre_\(Columbus_Circle\) "Majestic Theatre (Columbus Circle)") (1953) and then at the [NBC Century Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Century_Theatre "New Century Theatre"), after which the ceremony took place solely in Los Angeles. In 1961, the Oscars moved to the [Santa Monica Civic Auditorium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Monica_Civic_Auditorium "Santa Monica Civic Auditorium") in [Santa Monica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Monica,_California "Santa Monica, California"), California. In 1969, the Academy moved the ceremonies back to Downtown Los Angeles, to the [Dorothy Chandler Pavilion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dorothy_Chandler_Pavilion "Dorothy Chandler Pavilion") at the [Los Angeles County Music Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Music_Center "Los Angeles Music Center"). In the late 1990s and early 2000s, the ceremony returned to the Shrine Auditorium.

In 2002, Hollywood's [Dolby Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dolby_Theatre "Dolby Theatre"), previously known as the Kodak Theatre, became the presentation's current venue.[\[107\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-109)

In the first year of the awards, the Best Directing award was split into two categories, Drama and Comedy. At times, the Best Original Score award has also been split into separate categories, Drama and Comedy/Musical. From the 1930s to the 1960s, the Art Direction (now Production Design), Cinematography, and Costume Design awards were split into two categories (black-and-white films and color films). Prior to 2012, the Production Design award was called Art Direction, while the Makeup and Hairstyling award was called Makeup.

In August 2018, the Academy announced that several categories would not be televised live, but recorded during commercial breaks and aired later in the ceremony.[\[109\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-111) Following dissent from Academy members, they announced that they would air all 24 categories live. This followed several proposals, among them, the introduction of a Popular Film category, that the Academy had announced but did not implement.[\[110\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-112)
